--- PAGE 329 ---
ASSISTANT
Both behaviours matter for the tests:
- **uvicorn** runs the app's startup (including migrations) *before* it creates its listening socket, at least in a 
single process. With `--reload` or `--workers`, a parent process binds the socket first.
- **Alembic** does no commit of its own inside an existing transaction. So the advisory lock, the schema 
changes, the seed data and the version row all commit or roll back together.
One more concern while reading `run_migrations`. It takes a *transaction-level* lock as the first statement of its 
transaction. Under PostgreSQL's default READ COMMITTED isolation, every later statement sees fresh data, so a 
waiting worker sees the first worker's committed migration. But if a database or role is configured with 
`default_transaction_isolation = 'repeatable read'`, which some deployments do, the transaction's snapshot is 
taken at the lock statement, *before* the wait. The waiting worker would then see the database as it was before 
the other worker migrated it. I'll check this with a quick experiment in a throwaway schema. Four threads will 
block on the lock together, then be released at once:
Tool Use: bash
command

--- PAGE 331 ---
ASSISTANT
**Defect confirmed.** Under READ COMMITTED, all 4 workers start and the database ends at version 0002 with 
115 products. Under REPEATABLE READ or SERIALIZABLE, **3 of the 4 workers crash** with `relation 
"alembic_version" already exists`. Each waited for the lock but then didn't see the migration the first worker had 
committed, so it tried to migrate again. The database itself ends up correct; the failure is in the waiting workers' 
startup. That contradicts the README's claim that concurrent workers are serialized safely.
One result is an artifact of my experiment, not an app defect. The `KeyError: 'config'` comes from Alembic's 
process-global `context` object, which isn't thread-safe, and the app runs migrations once per *process*. So the 
real tests will use **separate uvicorn processes**, which is how workers actually start.
Plan, following your instructions:
1. **Shared fixtures in `tests/conftest.py`:**
   - a blank, isolated test schema, with the existing `database` fixture rebuilt on top of it;
   - a factory that starts uvicorn processes with a log file each and always stops them;
   - a fixture that holds the migration lock, so a test can decide exactly when startup may proceed.
2. **PostgreSQL startup tests in `tests/test_startup.py`:** fresh database, repeated restarts with edited and deleted 
products, upgrading from the first release with user products, concurrent startup under each isolation level, no 
requests before migrations finish, and migration failure with rollback.
3. **Run them *before* any fix**, so the REPEATABLE READ and SERIALIZABLE concurrency cases fail.
4. **Fix `migration.py` only.** Applied migrations stay unchanged and no features are added.
First, the fixtures:

class IsolatedSchema(SimpleNamespace):
    """A uniquely named, initially empty schema in the test database."""
    def app_url(self, *settings, application_name=None):
        """DATABASE_URL for an application process that should use this schema.

def alembic_config():
    return Config(str(ROOT / "alembic.ini"))
def migrate(engine, revision="head"):
    """Upgrade a schema in-process, as a previous deployment would have."""
    config = alembic_config()
    with engine.connect() as connection:
        config.attributes["connection"] = connection
        command.upgrade(config, revision)
    return config

def free_port():
    with socket.socket() as probe:
        probe.bind(("127.0.0.1", 0))
        return probe.getsockname()[1]
class AppServer:
    """The application started the way users start it: uvicorn in its own process."""
    def __init__(self, database_url, log_path, args=()):
        self.port = free_port()
        self.base_url = f"http://127.0.0.1:{self.port}"
        self.log_path = log_path
        self._log = open(log_path, "wb")
        self.process = subprocess.Popen(
            [sys.executable, "-m", "uvicorn", "inventory_management_service.main:app",
             "--host", "127.0.0.1", "--port", str(self.port), *args],
            cwd=ROOT, env={**os.environ, "DATABASE_URL": database_url},
            stdout=self._log, stderr=subprocess.STDOUT,
        )
    @property
    def output(self):
        return self.log_path.read_text(encoding="utf-8", errors="replace")
    def accepts_connections(self):
        try:
11/6/26, 11:31 PM live-compare-d669c4bf__cont12-4c5ba0d4 — transcript
about:blank 333/393

--- PAGE 334 ---
            socket.create_connection(("127.0.0.1", self.port), timeout=0.5).close()
            return True
        except OSError:
            return False
    def wait_until_serving(self, timeout=60):
        deadline = time.monotonic() + timeout
        while True:
            assert self.process.poll() is None, f"the server exited during startup:\\n{self.output}"
            try:
                if httpx.get(f"{self.base_url}/openapi.json", timeout=2).status_code == 200:
                    return
            except httpx.TransportError:
                pass
            assert time.monotonic() < deadline, f"the server did not start within 
{timeout}s:\\n{self.output}"
            time.sleep(0.1)
    def wait_for_exit(self, timeout=60):
        try:
            return self.process.wait(timeout=timeout)
        except subprocess.TimeoutExpired:
            pytest.fail(f"the server did not exit within {timeout}s:\\n{self.output}")
    def stop(self):
        if self.process.poll() is None:
            self.process.terminate()
            try:
                self.process.wait(timeout=15)
            except subprocess.TimeoutExpired:
                self.process.kill()
                self.process.wait()
        self._log.close()
@pytest.fixture
def app_servers(tmp_path):
    """Start application processes with start(database_url, *uvicorn_args); all are stopped 
afterwards."""
    started = []
    def start(database_url, *args):
        server = AppServer(database_url, tmp_path / f"server-{len(started)}.log", args)
        started.append(server)
        if VERIFICATION is not None:
            VERIFICATION.servers.append(server)
        return server
    try:
        yield start
    finally:
        for server in started:
            server.stop()

    from inventory_management_service.migration import MIGRATION_LOCK

    connection.execute(text("SELECT pg_advisory_lock(:key)"), {"key": MIGRATION_LOCK})

    def waiting(application_name):
        """Number of sessions of that application waiting for the migration lock."""
        return connection.scalar(text("""
            SELECT count(*) FROM pg_locks l JOIN pg_stat_activity a ON a.pid = l.pid
            WHERE l.locktype = 'advisory' AND NOT l.granted AND a.application_name = :name
              AND l.database = (SELECT oid FROM pg_database WHERE datname = current_database())
        """), {"name": application_name})
    def wait_for_waiters(application_name, count, timeout=60):
        deadline = time.monotonic() + timeout
        while waiting(application_name) < count:
            assert time.monotonic() < deadline, f"{count} startups did not reach the migration lock"
            time.sleep(0.05)
    held = {"value": True}
    def release():
        if held["value"]:
            connection.execute(text("SELECT pg_advisory_unlock(:key)"), {"key": MIGRATION_LOCK})
            connection.commit()
            held["value"] = False
    try:
        yield SimpleNamespace(wait_for_waiters=wait_for_waiters, release=release)
    finally:
        release()
        connection.close()

"""Automatic startup migrations.
The first two tests use an in-process client with the migration step replaced. The
others start the real application (uvicorn in its own process, as users run it) against
an isolated schema in the test database and check the database it leaves behind. A
fixture can hold the application's migration lock, so the tests decide exactly when a
startup may proceed and can observe the application while it waits.
"""
import re
import time
from pathlib import Path
from uuid import uuid7
import httpx
import pytest
from alembic.config import Config
from alembic.script import ScriptDirectory
from fastapi.testclient import TestClient
from sqlalchemy import text
from inventory_management_service.main import PRODUCTS, app
ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1]
HEAD = ScriptDirectory.from_config(Config(str(ROOT / "alembic.ini"))).get_current_head()
# The sample products exactly as the applied seed migration inserts them: SKU -> id.
SEEDS = dict(
    (sku, product_id) for product_id, sku in re.findall(
        r"\('([0-9a-f-]{36})'::uuid, '([A-Z0-9._-]+)'",
        (ROOT / "migrations" / "sql" / "0002_sample_products.sql").read_text(encoding="utf-8"),
    )
)

calls.append("migrated"))
    with TestClient(app) as client:
        assert calls == ["migrated"]
        assert client.get("/docs").status_code == 200

def test_startup_stops_if_migration_fails(monkeypatch):
    def fail():
        raise RuntimeError("Database unavailable")
    monkeypatch.setattr("inventory_management_service.main.run_migrations", fail)
    with pytest.raises(RuntimeError, match="Database unavailable"):
        with TestClient(app):
            pytest.fail("The application must not start after a migration failure")
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Helpers for the PostgreSQL startup tests
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
def table_exists(connection, name):
    return connection.scalar(text("SELECT to_regclass(:name) IS NOT NULL"), {"name": name})
def versions(engine):
    """Rows of alembic_version, or [] if the schema was never migrated."""
    with engine.connect() as connection:
        if not table_exists(connection, "alembic_version"):
            return []
        return connection.scalars(text("SELECT version_num FROM alembic_version")).all()
def product_rows(engine):
    """Every stored product row, complete, in id order."""
    with engine.connect() as connection:
        return [tuple(row) for row in connection.execute(text("SELECT * FROM products ORDER BY id"))]
def sku_counts(engine):
    with engine.connect() as connection:
        return dict(connection.execute(text("SELECT sku, count(*) FROM products GROUP BY sku")).all())
def product_count(server):
    response = httpx.get(f"{server.base_url}{PRODUCTS}", params={"size": 1}, timeout=10)
    assert response.status_code == 200, response.text
    return response.json()["totalItems"]

        stored = dict(connection.execute(text("SELECT sku, id::text FROM products")).all())
        states = connection.execute(text(
            "SELECT DISTINCT status, deleted_at IS NULL, version FROM products"
        )).all()
    assert len(SEEDS) == 115

@pytest.mark.postgres
def test_restarts_preserve_products_and_never_reseed(blank_database, app_servers):
    engine = blank_database.engine
    first = app_servers(blank_database.app_url())
    first.wait_until_serving()
    created = httpx.post(f"{first.base_url}{PRODUCTS}", timeout=10, json={
        "sku": "user-1", "name": "User product", "category": "TOOLS",
        "price": {"amount": "12.3400", "currency": "EUR"}, "unitOfMeasure": "PIECE", "attributes": {},
    })
    assert created.status_code == 201, created.text
    first.stop()
    # Edits made while the application was down: an edited sample, a soft-deleted
    # sample, and a sample removed entirely.
    with engine.begin() as connection:
        connection.execute(text("""
            UPDATE products SET name = 'Edited laptop', price_amount = 1.2345, version = 4,
                   updated_at = updated_at + interval '1 day'
            WHERE sku = 'DEMO-LAPTOP-001'
        """))
        connection.execute(text("""
            UPDATE products SET status = 'DELETED', deleted_at = updated_at WHERE sku = 'DEMO-MOUSE-
001'
        """))
        connection.execute(text("DELETE FROM products WHERE sku = 'DEMO-KEYBOARD-001'"))
    before = product_rows(engine)
    for _ in range(2):
        server = app_servers(blank_database.app_url())
        server.wait_until_serving()
        assert versions(engine) == [HEAD]
        assert product_rows(engine) == before      # ids, edited values, and deletions unchanged
        server.stop()
    counts = sku_counts(engine)
    assert set(counts.values()) == {1}
    assert "DEMO-KEYBOARD-001" not in counts       # a removed sample is not re-seeded
    assert len(counts) == 115                      # 114 samples + 1 user product
    server = app_servers(blank_database.app_url())
    server.wait_until_serving()
    edited = httpx.get(f"{server.base_url}{PRODUCTS}/{SEEDS['DEMO-LAPTOP-001']}", timeout=10).json()
    assert (edited["name"], edited["price"]["amount"], edited["version"]) == ("Edited laptop", 1.2345, 
4)
    assert httpx.get(f"{server.base_url}{PRODUCTS}/{SEEDS['DEMO-MOUSE-001']}", timeout=10).status_code 
== 404
    assert httpx.get(created.headers["location"].replace(first.base_url, server.base_url), 
timeout=10).status_code == 200

    engine = blank_database.engine

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Concurrency and ordering
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# A database or role can change the default isolation level; startup must still work.
ISOLATION = [None, "default_transaction_isolation=repeatable read", 
"default_transaction_isolation=serializable"]
@pytest.mark.postgres
@pytest.mark.parametrize("isolation", ISOLATION, ids=["server-default", "repeatable-read", 
"serializable"])
def test_concurrent_startups_migrate_exactly_once(blank_database, app_servers, migration_lock, 
isolation):
    name = "inventra-concurrent-startup"
    url = blank_database.app_url(*filter(None, [isolation]), application_name=name)
    servers = [app_servers(url) for _ in range(4)]
    # All four reach the migration lock before any of them may migrate.
    migration_lock.wait_for_waiters(name, 4)
    assert not any(server.accepts_connections() for server in servers)
    migration_lock.release()
    for server in servers:
        server.wait_until_serving()
    assert versions(blank_database.engine) == [HEAD]
    assert sku_counts(blank_database.engine) == dict.fromkeys(SEEDS, 1)
    assert [product_count(server) for server in servers] == [115] * 4

@pytest.mark.parametrize("args", [(), ("--workers", "2")], ids=["single-process", "two-workers"])

def test_no_request_is_answered_before_migrations_finish(blank_database, app_servers, migration_lock, 
args):
    name = "inventra-ordering"
    server = app_servers(blank_database.app_url(application_name=name), *args)
    migration_lock.wait_for_waiters(name, 2 if args else 1)
    # Startup is blocked inside the migration. For several seconds, no request gets an
    # answer: a single process is not listening yet; with workers, the parent holds the
    # socket but no worker serves it.
    deadline = time.monotonic() + 3
    while time.monotonic() < deadline:
        assert server.process.poll() is None
        with pytest.raises(httpx.TransportError):
            httpx.get(f"{server.base_url}{PRODUCTS}", timeout=1)
    with blank_database.engine.connect() as connection:
        assert not table_exists(connection, "products")
    migration_lock.release()
    server.wait_until_serving()
    assert product_count(server) == 115
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Migration failure
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
def prepare_conflicting_table(engine):
    """A products table the migrations did not create (e.g. from another tool)."""
    with engine.begin() as connection:
        connection.execute(text("CREATE TABLE products (id uuid PRIMARY KEY, sku varchar(64))"))
        connection.execute(text("INSERT INTO products VALUES ('00000000-0000-7000-8000-000000000001', 
'LEGACY-1')"))
    return 'relation "products" already exists'
def prepare_seed_conflict(engine):
    """Release 0001 with a product that reuses a sample's id: the seed (0002) must fail."""
    from conftest import migrate
    migrate(engine, "0001")
    with engine.begin() as connection:
        connection.execute(text("""
            INSERT INTO products (id, sku, name, category, price_amount, price_currency, 
unit_of_measure,
                                  attributes, status, created_at, updated_at)
            VALUES (:id, 'USER-CLASH', 'Clash', 'MINE', 1, 'EUR', 'PIECE', '{}', 'ACTIVE', now(), 
now())
        """), {"id": SEEDS["DEMO-LAPTOP-001"]})
    return 'duplicate key value violates unique constraint "pk_products"'
@pytest.mark.postgres

                         ids=["conflicting-table", "seed-conflict-after-0001"])

    engine = blank_database.engine
    expected_error = prepare(engine)
    before_versions, before_rows = versions(engine), product_rows(engine)
    server = app_servers(blank_database.app_url())
    listened = False
    while server.process.poll() is None:

ASSISTANT
Found it, and it's a bug in my test fixture. All four startups are waiting on the lock with the right 
`application_name`. But my lock-holding session sits "idle in transaction", and PostgreSQL caches 
`pg_stat_activity` for the rest of a transaction (`stats_fetch_consistency = cache`). So my counting query kept 
seeing its first, empty snapshot.
The fix is to end the transaction after each count. First I'll clean up the stuck run. Its uvicorn processes and 
schema are ones this run created, so I'll stop exactly those PIDs and drop only `test_` schemas. There were none 
before the run.
Tool Use: bash
command
PYTEST_PID=$(pgrep -f "pytest tests/test_startup.py"); SERVERS=$(pgrep -f "uvicorn 
inventory_management_service.main:app --host 127.0.0.1 --port"); echo "pytest: $PYTEST_PID  servers 
started by it: $SERVERS"
kill $PYTEST_PID; sleep 1; kill $SERVERS 2>/dev/null; sleep 2; pgrep -fa "uvicorn inventory|pytest 
tests" || echo "all stopped"
PGPASSWORD=postgres psql -h 127.0.0.1 -U postgres -d inventra_test -Atc "SELECT nspname FROM 
pg_namespace WHERE nspname LIKE 'test\_%'" | while read s; do echo "dropping leftover test schema $s"; 
PGPASSWORD=postgres psql -h 127.0.0.1 -U postgres -d inventra_test -qc "DROP SCHEMA \"$s\" CASCADE"; 
done
cd /workspace/inventory-management-service && python3 - <<'EOF'
p = "tests/conftest.py"; s = open(p).read()
old = '''    def waiting(application_name):
        """Number of sessions of that application waiting for the migration lock."""
        return connection.scalar(text("""
            SELECT count(*) FROM pg_locks l JOIN pg_stat_activity a ON a.pid = l.pid
            WHERE l.locktype = 'advisory' AND NOT l.granted AND a.application_name = :name
              AND l.database = (SELECT oid FROM pg_database WHERE datname = current_database())
        """), {"name": application_name})
'''
new = '''    def waiting(application_name):
        """Number of sessions of that application waiting for the migration lock."""
        count = connection.scalar(text("""
            SELECT count(*) FROM pg_locks l JOIN pg_stat_activity a ON a.pid = l.pid
            WHERE l.locktype = 'advisory' AND NOT l.granted AND a.application_name = :name
              AND l.database = (SELECT oid FROM pg_database WHERE datname = current_database())
        """), {"name": application_name})
        # End the transaction: PostgreSQL keeps pg_stat_activity fixed within one.
        connection.commit()
        return count
'''
assert s.count(old) == 1; open(p, "w").write(s.replace(old, new))
EOF
echo fixed

--- PAGE 351 ---
ASSISTANT
**The defect is demonstrated.** When the database's default isolation is REPEATABLE READ or SERIALIZABLE, a 
worker that waited on the migration lock exits with `DuplicateTable: relation "alembic_version" already exists`. Its 
transaction snapshot was taken at the lock statement, before the first worker committed.
**The fix:** run the startup migration transaction at READ COMMITTED regardless of the database default. Every 
statement after the lock then sees the committed state. This changes only `run_migrations()`; the migrations and 
the API are untouched.
Tool Use: bash
command
cd /workspace/inventory-management-service && cp inventory_management_service/migration.py 
/tmp/migration.before_fix.py && python3 - <<'EOF'
p = "inventory_management_service/migration.py"; s = open(p).read()
old = '''    with engine().begin() as connection:
        # Concurrent application workers must not migrate the database together.
        connection.execute(text("SELECT pg_advisory_xact_lock(:key)"), {"key": MIGRATION_LOCK})
        config.attributes["connection"] = connection
        command.upgrade(config, "head")
'''
new = '''    with engine().connect() as connection:
        # Migrate at READ COMMITTED whatever the database or role default. Under
        # REPEATABLE READ or SERIALIZABLE, the snapshot would be taken by the lock
        # statement, before waiting, so a process that waited for another one would not
        # see its committed migration and would try to apply it again.
        connection.execution_options(isolation_level="READ COMMITTED")
        with connection.begin():
            # Concurrent application workers must not migrate the database together.
            connection.execute(text("SELECT pg_advisory_xact_lock(:key)"), {"key": MIGRATION_LOCK})
            config.attributes["connection"] = connection
            command.upgrade(config, "head")
'''
assert s.count(old) == 1; open(p, "w").write(s.replace(old, new))
EOF
diff /tmp/migration.before_fix.py inventory_management_service/migration.py; git diff --stat -- 
migrations/ | tail -1; echo "(no changes under migrations/ if the line above is empty)"

ASSISTANT
All 12 pass after the fix, and the run left no processes or schemas. Before trusting the new tests, one robustness 
gap: the migration-failure test loops `while the server is running` with no deadline. If a regression made startup 
*swallow* migration errors, the server would keep running and the test would hang rather than fail. I'll bound it:
Tool Use: bash
command
cd /workspace/inventory-management-service && python3 - <<'EOF'
p = "tests/test_startup.py"; s = open(p).read()
old = '''    server = app_servers(blank_database.app_url())
    listened = False
    while server.process.poll() is None:
        listened = listened or server.accepts_connections()
        time.sleep(0.05)
    assert server.wait_for_exit() != 0
'''
new = '''    server = app_servers(blank_database.app_url())
    listened = False
    deadline = time.monotonic() + 60
    while server.process.poll() is None and time.monotonic() < deadline:
        listened = listened or server.accepts_connections()
        time.sleep(0.05)
    assert server.process.poll() is not None, f"startup did not stop after the 
failure:\\n{server.output}"
    assert server.wait_for_exit() != 0
'''
assert s.count(old) == 1; open(p, "w").write(s.replace(old, new))
EOF
echo ok

## Startup migrations and sample products
Application startup runs `alembic upgrade head` before accepting requests. Migration failures stop 
startup. Concurrent application workers serialize startup migrations with a PostgreSQL advisory lock. 
The database role must have permission to apply schema changes.
Revision `0002` executes `migrations/sql/0002_sample_products.sql`, inserting all 115 sample products 
(the original 15 plus 100 additional color variants). The SQL uses `ON CONFLICT (sku) DO NOTHING`, 
preserving existing product data. Alembic records the revision so later starts do not seed again. This 
seeds every database upgraded to head, including new databases. Downgrading to `0001` preserves seed 
rows because they may have been edited or referenced; downgrading to base drops the products table.
The catalog is also available in `data/sample-products.json`, with a standalone copy of the insert 
script at `data/sample-products.sql`. Keep the versioned migration SQL unchanged after deployment; add 
a new migration for future catalog changes. Include the `migrations` directory and `alembic.ini` when 
deploying the Python application.
To apply migrations without starting the API:
```powershell
.\.venv\Scripts\python.exe -m alembic upgrade head
.\.venv\Scripts\python.exe -m alembic current
```
## Create a product

ASSISTANT
Tool Use: bash
command
cd /workspace/inventory-management-service && python3 - <<'EOF'
p = "README.md"; s = open(p).read()
old = '''Application startup runs `alembic upgrade head` before accepting requests. Migration failures 
stop startup. Concurrent application workers serialize startup migrations with a PostgreSQL advisory 
lock. The database role must have permission to apply schema changes.
'''
new = '''Application startup runs `alembic upgrade head` before accepting requests. The database role 
must have permission to apply schema changes.
- **Ordering.** No request is answered until the migrations have committed. A single uvicorn process 
opens its port only after startup. With `--workers` or `--reload`, the parent process holds the port 
earlier, but requests wait until a worker has finished starting.
- **Failure.** If a migration fails, the whole upgrade rolls back (all revisions run in one 
transaction), the process exits with a non-zero status, and the port is never opened. For example, a 
database with a `products` table that these migrations did not create fails with `relation "products" 
already exists` and is left unchanged.
- **Concurrency.** Concurrent application processes serialize startup migrations with a PostgreSQL 
advisory lock. Exactly one applies them, and the others find the database at head. The migration 
transaction always runs at READ COMMITTED, even if the database or role defaults to REPEATABLE READ or 
SERIALIZABLE. With those defaults, a waiting process would not see the migration committed while it 
waited, and would fail at startup.
- **Repeated startups** change nothing: existing product IDs, edited values, soft deletions, and 
removed sample products stay as they are.
'''
assert s.count(old) == 1; s = s.replace(old, new)
old = '''- `tests/test_startup.py` checks that startup runs the migrations and stops when they 
fail.'''
new = '''- `tests/test_startup.py` checks that startup runs the migrations and stops when they fail. 
Its PostgreSQL tests start the real application (uvicorn in separate processes) against isolated 
schemas. They cover a fresh database, repeated restarts after products were edited, soft-deleted, or 
removed, an upgrade from release `0001` that already holds products (including one with a sample SKU), 
four concurrent startups under each isolation level, that no request is answered while migrations are 
in progress (one process and `--workers 2`), and failed migrations that roll back without opening the 
port. A fixture holds the migration lock so that the tests control when startup may proceed.'''
assert s.count(old) == 1; s = s.replace(old, new)
open(p, "w").write(s)
EOF

--- PAGE 362 ---
ASSISTANT
I found and fixed one real defect in startup migrations: concurrent startups fail if the database defaults to a 
stricter isolation level (REPEATABLE READ or SERIALIZABLE). The new tests show it failing before the fix and 
passing after. The full verification now passes: exit 0, 314 tests, including 54 PostgreSQL tests (10 of them new), 
none skipped. The development database was identical before and after, and no test schemas or processes were 
left behind. No migration or data files were changed.
## The defect
Concurrent startups take turns using a database lock so only one migrates. The lock request is the first statement 
of the migration transaction. Under REPEATABLE READ or SERIALIZABLE, that first statement also fixes what the 
transaction can see. A worker that waited therefore couldn't see the migration another worker had just 
committed, tried to apply it again, and crashed with `relation "alembic_version" already exists`. In 4-way 
concurrent startups, 3 of the 4 workers crashed; under PostgreSQL's normal default (READ COMMITTED) all 4 
started. The database itself ended up correct.
**Failing test first:** `test_concurrent_startups_migrate_exactly_once` failed for repeatable read and serializable, 
with that error in the server log, while the other 10 new tests passed.
**Fix (`migration.py` only):** the migration transaction now always runs at READ COMMITTED, whatever the 
database default. After the fix all 12 startup tests pass. I also moved the lock key into a named constant, without 
changing behaviour.
## New tests
These start the real application in separate processes, each against its own empty test schema. To control timing, 
a fixture holds the migration lock so a test can watch the app while it waits.
| Scenario | What's checked |
|---|---|
| Fresh database | Migrated to the latest version; exactly the 115 sample products, with the IDs fixed in the seed 
SQL |
| Repeated restarts (twice) | After editing one sample, soft-deleting another and removing a third, every stored 
row is unchanged, including user-created products; the removed sample is not re-added; no SKU is duplicated |
| Upgrade from the first release | Existing user products, including one with a sample SKU, are untouched; that 
SKU isn't duplicated; the other 114 samples are added with their fixed IDs |
| 4 concurrent startups | All 4 wait on the lock and none accepts connections; after release all serve; migrated 
exactly once; under normal, repeatable read and serializable settings |
| No requests before migrations finish | While migration is blocked, no request gets an answer for 3 seconds and 
nothing is created in the database; with one process and with `--workers 2` |
| Migration failure | A pre-existing `products` table, or a seed conflict after the first release: the process exits with 
an error, never opens its port, and the database is left exactly as before |
**Mutation check:** I ran six deliberate breakages, and each was caught by the intended test:
- serving before migrations finish;
- swallowing migration errors;
- re-adding samples on every startup;
- stopping short of the latest version;
- removing the lock;
- seeding without skipping existing SKUs (applied to the seed file only temporarily, then restored and verified 
unchanged).

| OpenAPI description (`main.py`) | "Sorting is stable … pages neither overlap nor skip products." | **Overbroad**:

| README, List products | "never repeats or skips … while the data is unchanged" | Correct as far as it goes |
| README, same paragraph | "Offset paging can shift by a row if products are created or deleted" | 
**Understated**: it can shift by as many rows as changed, and sort-key updates and filter-membership changes 
shift pages too |
| README | "The count and the page are read from one snapshot, so they agree even while products are being

commit *within* one request is invisible. |
| Field description ("Ties are broken by id") | Accurate | no change |
I'll add controlled PostgreSQL tests in three groups:
1. **Unchanged data:** the existing every-sort test already covers deterministic ordering, so I'll keep it.
2. **Changes between page requests:** creations, soft deletions, sort-key updates, non-sort updates and filter-
membership changes, applied between page 1 and page 2. Each scenario checks two things: the page equals the 
slice of the *current* ordering, which is what offset pagination actually guarantees, and the exact repeat or 
omission that results. A walk that changes data before every page checks the slice property and the totals 
throughout.
3. **Changes within one request:** a commit from another connection injected *between* the count query and 
the page query, to test the snapshot claim directly.

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Offset pagination while the data changes
#
# What offset pagination guarantees: each response is computed afresh as the slice
# [(page - 1) * size, page * size) of the current ordering of the products that are
# not soft-deleted, and its totals describe that same state. Between requests nothing
# is pinned, so changes before the current position make later pages repeat or omit
# products. These tests pin down both the guarantee and the exact anomalies.
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
NAME_ORDER = "SELECT sku FROM products WHERE deleted_at IS NULL ORDER BY name, id"
def name_order(engine):
    """The current ordering for sort=name, computed independently of the API."""
    with engine.connect() as connection:
        return connection.scalars(text(NAME_ORDER)).all()
def page_of(client, page, size=10, **params):
    response = client.get(PRODUCTS, params={"page": page, "size": size, "sort": "name"} | params)
    assert response.status_code == 200, response.text
    body = response.json()

    return skus(body["items"]), body
def create_named(client, payload, sku, name):
    assert client.post(PRODUCTS, json=payload | {"sku": sku, "name": name}).status_code == 201
def soft_delete(engine, sku):
    with engine.begin() as connection:
        connection.execute(text(
            "UPDATE products SET status = 'DELETED', deleted_at = updated_at WHERE sku = :sku"
        ), {"sku": sku})
def update(engine, sku, **values):
    assignments = ", ".join(f"{column} = :{column}" for column in values)
    with engine.begin() as connection:
        connection.execute(text(
            f"UPDATE products SET {assignments}, version = version + 1, "
            "updated_at = updated_at + interval '1 second' WHERE sku = :sku"
        ), {"sku": sku, **values})
# Each scenario: a change made between the page 1 and page 2 requests (size 10,
# sort=name), and the page 2 that results, in terms of the original order `o`.
PAGE_CHANGES = {
    "create-sorting-before": (
        lambda engine, client, payload, o: create_named(client, payload, "NEW-1", "000 Early 
product"),
        lambda o: [o[9]] + o[10:19],                  # the last item of page 1 repeats
    ),
    "three-creates-sorting-before": (
        lambda engine, client, payload, o: [
            create_named(client, payload, f"NEW-{n}", f"000 Early product {n}") for n in range(3)
        ],
        lambda o: o[7:10] + o[10:17],                 # the shift equals the number of changes
    ),
    "create-sorting-after": (
        lambda engine, client, payload, o: create_named(client, payload, "NEW-1", "zzz Late product"),
        lambda o: o[10:20],                           # no effect on the next page
    ),
    "soft-delete-seen": (
        lambda engine, client, payload, o: soft_delete(engine, o[0]),
        lambda o: o[11:21],                           # o[10] is never shown
    ),
    "soft-delete-not-yet-seen": (
        lambda engine, client, payload, o: soft_delete(engine, o[14]),
        lambda o: o[10:14] + o[15:21],                # it disappears; nothing else is affected
    ),
    "update-moves-seen-product-later": (
        lambda engine, client, payload, o: update(engine, o[0], name="zzz Renamed"),
        lambda o: o[11:21],                           # o[10] is skipped; o[0] will be shown again
    ),
    "update-moves-unseen-product-earlier": (
        lambda engine, client, payload, o: update(engine, o[12], name="000 Renamed"),
        lambda o: [o[9]] + o[10:12] + o[13:19],       # o[9] repeats; o[12] is never shown
    ),
    "update-without-sort-key-change": (
        lambda engine, client, payload, o: [
            update(engine, sku, description="Changed") for sku in (o[0], o[12])
        ],

        lambda o: o[10:20],                           # no effect
    ),

@pytest.mark.parametrize("scenario", list(PAGE_CHANGES))
def test_changes_between_page_requests(database, client, payload, scenario):
    engine, _ = database
    change, expected_second_page = PAGE_CHANGES[scenario]
    original = name_order(engine)
    first, _ = page_of(client, 1)
    assert first == original[0:10]
    change(engine, client, payload, original)
    second, body = page_of(client, 2)
    # The guarantee: the slice of the current ordering, with totals for the current state.
    current = name_order(engine)
    assert second == current[10:20]
    assert (body["totalItems"], body["totalPages"]) == (len(current), -(-len(current) // 10))
    # The consequence for a client paging through: the exact repeat or omission.
    assert second == expected_second_page(original)
def test_filter_membership_change_between_requests(database, client):
    """A product that stops matching a filter behaves like a deletion for later pages."""
    engine, _ = database
    params = {"maxPrice": "30", "sort": "price", "size": 5}
    order_sql = ("SELECT sku FROM products WHERE deleted_at IS NULL AND price_amount <= 30 "
                 "ORDER BY price_amount, id")
    with engine.connect() as connection:
        original = connection.scalars(text(order_sql)).all()
    first = skus(client.get(PRODUCTS, params=params | {"page": 1}).json()["items"])
    assert first == original[0:5]
    update(engine, original[0], price_amount=Decimal("99"))          # no longer matches maxPrice=30
    second = client.get(PRODUCTS, params=params | {"page": 2}).json()
    with engine.connect() as connection:
        current = connection.scalars(text(order_sql)).all()
    assert skus(second["items"]) == current[5:10] == original[6:11]  # original[5] is never shown
    assert second["totalItems"] == len(original) - 1
def test_every_page_reflects_the_data_at_request_time(database, client, payload):
    """Walk all pages while something changes before every request."""
    engine, _ = database
    changes = [
        lambda o: create_named(client, payload, "WALK-1", "000 Walk early"),
        lambda o: soft_delete(engine, o[3]),
        lambda o: update(engine, o[40], name="000 Walk renamed"),
        lambda o: create_named(client, payload, "WALK-2", "zzz Walk late"),
        lambda o: soft_delete(engine, o[-1]),
        lambda o: update(engine, o[5], description="Walk"),
        lambda o: soft_delete(engine, o[60]),
    ]
    page, shown = 1, []
    while True:
        current = name_order(engine)
        items, body = page_of(client, page, size=12)
        assert items == current[(page - 1) * 12:page * 12]
        assert body["totalItems"] == len(current)

        shown += items

            changes[page - 1](name_order(engine))
        page += 1

    # needs a complete pass must not change the data, or must tolerate both.
    final = name_order(engine)

    omitted = set(final) - set(shown)
    assert repeated and omitted
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Within one request: the count and the page come from one snapshot
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
def commit_before_page_query(engine, change):
    """Make `change` commit from another connection just before the next page query."""
    fired = []
    def before_cursor_execute(connection, cursor, statement, parameters, context, executemany):
        if not fired and "FROM products" in statement and "LIMIT" in statement:
            fired.append(True)          # set first: the change below also runs statements
            with engine.begin() as other:
                change(other)
    event.listen(engine, "before_cursor_execute", before_cursor_execute)
    return fired, lambda: event.remove(engine, "before_cursor_execute", before_cursor_execute)
SNAPSHOT_CHANGES = {
    "create": lambda connection: connection.execute(text("""
        INSERT INTO products (id, sku, name, category, price_amount, price_currency, unit_of_measure,
                              attributes, status, created_at, updated_at)
        VALUES (gen_random_uuid(), 'RACE-1', '000 Racing product', 'RACE', 1, 'EUR', 'PIECE', '{}',
                'ACTIVE', now(), now())
    """)),
    "soft-delete": lambda connection: connection.execute(text("""
        UPDATE products SET status = 'DELETED', deleted_at = updated_at
        WHERE id = (SELECT id FROM products ORDER BY name, id LIMIT 1)
    """)),
}
@pytest.mark.parametrize("scenario", list(SNAPSHOT_CHANGES))
def test_count_and_page_come_from_one_snapshot(database, client, scenario):
    engine, _ = database
    before = name_order(engine)
    fired, remove = commit_before_page_query(engine, SNAPSHOT_CHANGES[scenario])
    try:
        items, body = page_of(client, 2, size=100)
    finally:
        remove()
    assert fired, "the change was not injected between the count and the page query"
    # The change committed after the count but before the page query; neither sees it.
    assert body["totalItems"] == len(before) == 115
    assert items == before[100:115]
    assert len(items) == body["totalItems"] - 100

ASSISTANT
All 12 pass on the unchanged implementation. Together with the existing unchanged-data tests, that means the

Reviewing my snapshot test before the mutations, one assertion is stricter than the requirement. It demands that 
*neither* query sees the injected change (`totalItems == 115`, the "before" state). The requirement is only that 
the count and the page **agree**, i.e. describe one and the same state. If the service ever issued the page query 
first, both would legitimately see the change, and my test would wrongly fail. I'll relax it to "the response matches 
exactly one state: before or after", which still fails whenever the count and the page disagree:
Tool Use: bash
command

    # The change committed after the count but before the page query; neither sees it.
    assert body["totalItems"] == len(before) == 115
    assert items == before[100:115]
    assert len(items) == body["totalItems"] - 100
    # The next request sees the change.
    assert page_of(client, 2, size=100)[1]["totalItems"] == len(name_order(engine)) != len(before)
'''
new = '''    assert fired, "the change was not injected during the request"
    after = name_order(engine)
    assert len(after) != len(before)
    # The change committed between the two queries. The response must describe one state,
    # either before or after the change, never a count from one and items from the other.
    assert (body["totalItems"], items) in [(len(before), before[100:200]), (len(after), 
after[100:200])]
    assert len(items) == body["totalItems"] - 100
    # The next request sees the change.
    assert page_of(client, 2, size=100)[1]["totalItems"] == len(after)
'''

'''Within one response, the count and the page are read from one snapshot, so `totalItems`,

while the request runs.''')
edit("README.md", '''Sorting is stable: ties are always broken by product `id` in the same direction, 
so paging through a result never repeats or skips a product while the data is unchanged. This matters 
for the sample catalog because all 115 seeded products share one `createdAt`. Text sorting follows the 
database collation. Offset paging can shift by a row if products are created or deleted between page 
requests.''',
'''Ties in the sort order are always broken by product `id` in the same direction, so the order is 
total and deterministic. This matters for the sample catalog because all 115 seeded products share one 
`createdAt`. Text sorting follows the database collation.
Paging uses offsets. Page `n` returns rows `(n - 1) × size + 1` to `n × size` of the ordering as it is 
when that page is requested. Nothing is kept between requests. This guarantees:
- **Unchanged data:** paging from page 1 to `totalPages` returns every matching product exactly once, 
in order, and repeating a request returns the same page.
- **Changes between page requests:** pages can repeat or omit products, by as many positions as there 
were changes before the current position.
  - A product that *enters* the part already paged through moves later pages back, so the last product 
already shown appears again. It may have been created, updated to sort there, or updated to start 
11/6/26, 11:31 PM live-compare-d669c4bf__cont12-4c5ba0d4 — transcript
about:blank 376/393

--- PAGE 377 ---
matching the filters. If an update moved it there, it is not shown at all.
  - A product that *leaves* the part already paged through moves later pages forward, so the next 
product is omitted. It may have been soft-deleted, updated to sort later, or updated to stop matching 
the filters. If an update moved it later, it is shown again.
  - Changes that affect only later positions do not shift later pages, and neither do updates that 
change neither the sort key nor filter membership. With `sort=updatedAt`, every update changes the 
sort key.
  - `totalItems` and `totalPages` describe the data at each request, so they can change between pages.
A client that needs one complete pass without repeats or omissions must page through while the data 
does not change. Otherwise it should de-duplicate by `id` and accept that products can be missed. Gap-
free paging under concurrent changes would need keyset pagination, which is an API change.''')
edit("README.md", '''and UTC output under a non-UTC session time zone.''',
'''and UTC output under a non-UTC session time zone. For paging under change, they apply creations, 
soft deletions, sort-key updates, other updates, and filter-membership changes between page requests. 
Each resulting page must equal the slice of the current ordering, and the tests check the exact repeat 
or omission that follows. A commit injected between the count and the page query of one request shows 
that a response never mixes two states.''')

--- PAGE 380 ---
ASSISTANT
I found no code defect: the pagination works as intended, and the problem was documentation that promised 
more than offset pagination can deliver. The full verification passes (exit 0, 326 tests, 66 of them against 
PostgreSQL, none skipped). The development database was identical before and after, and no test schemas or 
processes were left.
## What offset pagination actually guarantees
- **Unchanged data:** sorting is deterministic because ties are broken by product `id`. Paging from page 1 to the 
last page returns every matching product exactly once, and repeating a request returns the same page. This was 
already tested for every sort field and direction.
- **Within one request:** the total count and the page of items describe the same state, even if another 
transaction commits between the two queries. This was previously only checked without a database; it's now 
shown against real PostgreSQL.
- **Between requests, nothing is kept:** each page is the slice of the ordering as it is at that moment, and the 
totals reflect that moment too. So pages can repeat or omit products.
## What the new tests show
Each scenario changes the data between page 1 and page 2. Every resulting page matched the slice of the 
current ordering, and the consequences were:
| Change between page requests | Effect on page 2 |
|---|---|
| Product created that sorts earlier (1, or 3) | The last 1 (or 3) products of page 1 appear again |
| Product created that sorts later | No effect |
| A product already shown is soft-deleted | The next product is never shown |
| A product not yet shown is soft-deleted | Only that product disappears |
| A product already shown is renamed to sort later | The next product is skipped; the renamed one appears again 
later |
| A product not yet shown is renamed to sort earlier | A product from page 1 repeats; the renamed one is never 
shown |
| Update that changes neither the sort key nor filter membership | No effect |
| A product already shown stops matching the price filter | Same as a deletion: the next product is omitted |
A longer test, which changed the data before every page request, also confirmed the totals follow the data and 
that both repeats and omissions occur across a full pass. For the within-request check, I inserted or soft-deleted a 
product from another connection exactly between the count and page queries. The response always described a 
single state.
## Mutation check
- **Removing the snapshot** that makes the count and page agree: now caught by a real-PostgreSQL test.
- **An off-by-one offset:** caught.
- **Removing the `id` tiebreaker:** caught.
- **Swapping the order of the count and page queries** (still consistent, so not a bug): correctly passes.
My first snapshot test was stricter than the requirement, so I relaxed it before running these mutations. One 
scenario initially failed only because my hand-written expected value was wrong; the underlying check passed, 
and I corrected the expectation.
## Documentation corrected
- **API description shown in Swagger:** it said pages "neither overlap nor skip products" with no condition. It 
11/6/26, 11:31 PM live-compare-d669c4bf__cont12-4c5ba0d4 — transcript
about:blank 380/393

--- PAGE 381 ---
now says that holds only while the data doesn't change, and that changes between requests can cause repeats or 
omissions. Only this text changed; the rest of the OpenAPI document is identical.
- **README:** "can shift by a row if products are created or deleted" understated the problem. It now explains 
the guarantees above: the shift equals the number of changes before the current position, and updates and filter 
changes count too. It also advises paging through unchanged data, or de-duplicating by `id` and accepting 
possible gaps. The within-request statement now covers deletions and changes, and the test description says 
"over unchanged data".
- **Code comments in `service.py`:** the same overbroad claim, corrected.
I added no cursor pagination; the README only notes that gap-free paging under concurrent changes would 
need keyset pagination, which would change the API.
## Not verified
- Real concurrent traffic; the changes were injected at controlled points.
- Other collations: the tests assume names starting "000" sort first and "zzz" last, which holds here (C.UTF-8) and 
in common collations.
- PostgreSQL 18 and Windows.

ASSISTANT (32)
Today the classification is consistent: all **66** tests that use a database fixture carry the `postgres` marker, and 
vice versa. But nothing enforces it, so a future unmarked database test could skip unnoticed. I'll close three gaps

1. **Enforce the classification at collection time, in every run.** A test that uses a database fixture without the 
`postgres` marker is a usage error (exit 4). That keeps `-m "not postgres"` truly database-independent and keeps 
the skip detection complete.
2. **In full verification, any skipped test fails the run**, not only marked ones. A skip means something in the full 
suite didn't run.
3. **Make the output unambiguous.** A passing verification prints its counts explicitly. A plain run that skipped 
PostgreSQL tests prints a warning saying it did *not* verify database behaviour, while keeping exit 0 as before.

## Gaps closed (in `tests/conftest.py` only)
- **Any skip now fails full verification.** Before, only tests marked `postgres` were counted, so a database-
dependent test without the marker could have skipped unnoticed.
- **Unmarked database tests are rejected.** Any run now exits 4 if a test uses a database fixture (`database`, 
`blank_database`, `migration_lock`) without `@pytest.mark.postgres`. This keeps the marker-based skip detection 
complete and `-m "not postgres"` free of database tests. Today all 66 database tests are correctly marked.
- **Clearer success line:** the summary now shows explicit counts, as in the table above.
## Checked against the current code
| Scenario | Exit | Output |
|---|---|---|
| A PostgreSQL test skips at runtime | **1** | `1 PostgreSQL integration test(s) were skipped: …test_currency_filter`,

| An unmarked database test is added (plain, unit-only and full runs) | **4** | `Tests that use a database fixture

| Unit-only run, `-m "not postgres"`, no database | 0 | `260 passed, 66 deselected`, with no warning, since
